Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 11406 ms + 911 ms + 8956 ms = 21273 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 9873 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 31145 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 21273 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 21273 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 30867 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5728 ms, comparable BL 2934 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 56601 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 8694 ms + 887 ms + 9167 ms = 18748 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 8519 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 27267 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 18748 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 18748 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 25860 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5693 ms, comparable BL 2835 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 52494 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 15030 ms + 900 ms + 11274 ms = 27204 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 4638 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 31842 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 27204 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 27204 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 30867 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5059 ms, comparable BL 2934 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 56601 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 17518 ms + 1009 ms + 12755 ms = 31282 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 4225 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 35507 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 31282 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 31282 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 26259 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5194 ms, comparable BL 3125 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 52494 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 9408 ms + 878 ms + 9448 ms = 19734 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 5747 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 25481 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 19734 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 19734 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 30867 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5651 ms, comparable BL 2934 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 56601 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 10568 ms + 1156 ms + 8627 ms = 20351 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 5616 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 25967 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 20351 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 20351 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 25860 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5710 ms, comparable BL 2835 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 52494 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 24306 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 24306 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 30196 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 4754 ms, comparable BL 2933 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 56601 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

ASSET WORK SUM
excludes duplicated pending waits		 8523 ms
actual init 433 ms · file work 8090 ms
excluded waits 14415 ms (247 ms pending init · 0 ms ready)
14 miss · 9 pending · 66 hit
14 paid fetches		 0 ms 12733 ms 21256 ms
35671 ms raw paused sum		 25969 ms
same TP-served rows
52494 ms all BL observed · 135 fetches		 107.38 MB
TP EFFECTIVE ASSET WAIT
incl. fall-through cost; not wall-clock		 21256 ms BL overlap-sum 25969 ms
REQUEST WINDOW
union of overlapping request waits		 5560 ms 2835 ms
same TP-served rows

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 8523 ms + 0 ms + 12733 ms = 21256 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 14415 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 35671 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 21256 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 21256 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 25969 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5560 ms, comparable BL 2835 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 52494 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Legend
  • Baseline — page run with recorded static assets fulfilled from the local isolated asset store. Live document/API requests may still go to origin.
  • Baseline-CDP — same CDP fulfill rig as TP for manifest assets, but bytes come from the recorded local asset store instead of live origin.
  • TokenPack — intercept + serve from packed PNG. Pays harness IPC tax on every served asset.
  • Δ TP vs BL (prod fairness) — TP minus baseline. Real comparison including harness handicap. If TP wins here, it wins despite the rig.
  • Δ TP vs BL-CDP (tech fairness) — TP minus baseline-CDP. Both pay harness tax, so this isolates pack-vs-direct. Tech-level win.
  • Est. prod TP (–harness) — projection of TP duration with harness IPC tax stripped out. Computed as TP × (1 − harnessRatio) where harnessRatio = sum(harness)/sum(totalMs) across all served-from-pack assets. Approximates a real Service-Worker deployment where the browser delivers pack bytes directly without CDP roundtrips. Estimate, not measurement.
